INTRODUCTION. 


I. 


The fifth and last Vaggo of the Samyutta-Nikāya, the 
so called Mahā-Vaggo, well deserves this name, being the 
largest of these great Divisions. It occupies, in the Singha- 
lese MS. of Copenhagen, 98 sheets (tr-ph]), in that of Paris, 
104 (nii-yo), in the Burmese MS. of Paris, 150 (tha-bhū), 
and in mine, 131. It consists of 12 Samyuttas, which 
added to the 44 of the preceding Divisions make up the 
total of 56. 

The following are the names of the 12 Samyuttas with 
the numbers of the Chapters and Suttas: 


Names Chapters  Suttas 

I. (XLV) Magga-S. ..... 8 180 
IL (XLVI) Bojhanga-S. . . .18 187: 
IIL (XLVII) Satipatthana-S. . . 10 108 
IV. (XLVIII) Indriya-8.. . . . 17 185 
V. (XLIX) Sammappadhana-S. 5 54 
VI. (L) Bala-S. ........ 10 110 
VIL (LI) Iddhipada-S. . . . .. 8 86 
VIIL (LII) Anuruddha-S. . . . . 2 24

The tiresome repetitions, peculiar to the buddhist script- 
ures) abound exceedingly i in the Maha-Vaggo, and form so 


1 No 175 (e 139), because 99—100 has been put (p. 135) 
"instead of 99—110. 


VI Introduction. 


great a proportion in several of its Samyuttas that im- 
portant abridgments are reguired. The singhalese and 
burmese MSS. differ so much in the manner and guantity 
of their abbreviation that they seem to have nothing com- 
mon, although they are dealing with the same subject. 
Moreover the reckoning of the chapters is, in several 
Samyuttas, subject to some difficulty. 


II. 

The first of these Samyuttas, the Magga, is divided in- 
to eight chapters, according to the burmese MS. of Paris 
(B5; but, between the fourth and the fifth of these chapters, 
five so called Peyyalas (Aññatitthiya, Suriya, two Eka- 
dhamma, Ganga) are inserted, apparently as a lengthening 
of the fourth chapter, but without any numeral designation. 
However, the first of them, the Annatitthiya is counted 
pancamo (fifth) in my MS. (B>), and, as the first chapter 
coming after the Peyyālas (the Appamāda) is also numbered 
paūcamo, we have, according to B*, two fifth chapters 
separated by four pseudo-chapters. Prudently, B* does 
not give any number either to the Peyyālas after the Aū- 
. fiatitthiya, or to the chapters (Vaggos) after the Appa- 

māda. More prudent still, the singhalese MSS. have no 
numbers for all the Peyyālas and Vaggos after what is 
in all the MSS. the fourth chapter. B: alone numbers the 
Vaggos, excluding the Peyyalas. — I followed it, but the 
reckoning seems to me by no means satisfactory. 

The last of the aforesaid Peyyālas, the Ganga, and the 
subsequent Vaggos (Appamāda, Balakarantya, Esana, Ogha) 
are the common share of the nine first Samyuttas, save 
the eighth, and form the last part of each of them. Three 
even of the Samyuttas, the fifth (Sammappadhāna), the 
sixth (Bala), and the nine (Jhāna) are nothing more than 
this very part, which cannot be rehearsed wholly each time. 
Therefore the burmese MSS. give only the first and the 
last sutta of the lump with reference to the first Samyutta 
(Magga), where the matter is developed at length; the 
Singhalese MSS. are less exact in reproducing the texts, 


Introduotion. VII 


but never fail to repeat the Uddānas, not however without 
some inaccuracy of which the trace is apparent in the 
present volume. I have combined the two systems as well 
as I could. As the subject can be considered under se- 
veral aspects, the aforesaid Uddanas occur twice in the 
Samyuttas II. IV. VI: why not in the others? I do not 
quite understand the cause of this difference; and so I have 
followed the MSS. 

The perplexity already alluded to as to the reckoning 
of the chapters occurs in other Samyuttas, especially in 
the last, where after the fourth chapter nothing is certain, 
the Singhalese MSS. noting two tatiyo vaggo separated by 
several chapters and all the MSS. giving at the end some 
titles without any precise application. I do not insist upon 
this difficulty; the reader will perceive it. 


HL 


The materials at my disposal for issuing this volume 
were as before: 

S*, the singhalese MS. of Copenhagen, for the loan of 
which I address once more my warm thanks to the Ad- 
ministration of the royal Library. This beautiful MS. 
has in this part an avowed gap, the fourth part of the 
sheet nai, the whole sheet no, and the first half of nau 
having been left blank. I . 

S3 the singhalese MS. of the Bibliothëque nationale 
(Paris). 

B: the burmese MS. of the same. 

B: burmese MS. belonging to me. It is unfortunately 
very deficient in this part, as many sheets are wanting. 
The four last ones, which are of another hand than the 
rest, were of little use on account of their extreme in- 
correctness. 

I have again to express my regret at being deprived 
of the use of S? the singhalese MS. of the British Museum, 
which I have only been able to consult on pages 1—144 
of the first volume (Sagātha-vaggo). I am also somewhat 
sorry at having had the Commentary only for the Sagātha- 


VIII Introduction. 


and Nidana-Vaggo, and in general for not having had at 
my disposal a greater number of MSS. (especially Siamese 
MSS., which were wanting altogether) I have endeavoured, 
as it is, to show the state of our MSS. So I have not 
inserted in the text any word not found in them. And 
I have given all the readings, although they were some- 
times evident blunders. Nevertheless I may have omitted 
some of them when they were not to be found in all the 
MSS. of same origin or when they were not repeated in 
similar passages, but this exceptionally and unwillingly. 

As the singhalese MSS. note abridgments by the sign 
pe, the burmese by la or pa, these tokens, in the present 
book, when isolated, are enough to assign abridgments to 
their respective MSS.; when joined, to show that the 
abridgments are common to all the MSS. Abridgments 
between brackets, and without either pe, la, or pa, exist 
in the MSS., but have none of those 3 marks. 


IV. 
With the Maha-Vaggo and this fifth volume, the Sam- 
yutta-Nikaya is at an end. Suming up the numbers of 
the Suttas in each great Division, I reach this result: 


I. Sagatha-Vaggo . . . . . 271 Suttas 
IL Nidàna-Vaggo . . . . . 286 , 
III. Khandha-Vaggo . . . . 788 , 
IV. Salayatana-Vaggo . . . . 391 , 
V. Mahā-Vaggo |. . . . .1908 , 
2889 


Now, the Samyutta-Nikaya is said to consist of 7,762 
-suttas as it is stated in the beginning of the Petavatthu 
atthakatha and very likely elsewhere: 

Oghataraņasuttādīni sattasuttasahassdni salta ca sata- 
suttāni dvāsatthi ca suttāni Samyuttanikayo. 

The difference is not small. But I already noted that 
there can be several modes of counting the suttas. For 
instance, in the Salayatana-Vaggo', I found 391 or 1850 


1 Samyutta-Nikaya part IV, P. XL XII. 


Introduction. IX 


suttas. The numerous suttas where the distinction of atita-, 
etarahi-, anāgata is made could each be counted perhaps 
as three texts. In this Maha-Vaggo itself, the ten suttas 
of the Appamada-Vaggo (p. 41—45), of the Esanā-Vaggo 
(p. 54—58) are divided each in four parts, which could 
be considered as so much suttas; and, this being repeated 
in eight Samyuttas, the total would be seriously increased. 
By these means, the sum of 7,762 can be attained, but 
not be got from the data of the MSS. I counted the 
suttas according to the Uddānas, and although I may 
have miscalculated to a certain extent, the true result is, 
on such a basis, certainly far below 7,762. 


V. 


I cannot discourse here upon the question whether 
the Samyutta Nikāya, seemingly in general, certainly in 
many instances, borrowed from other compilations. For 
example, in this volume, we have (p. 146) the fable of the 
hawk and the quail, and (p. 420) Gotama’s first Discourse 
styled here Tathagatenavutiam, the well known title Dham- 
macakkappavattanam being that of a chapter, not of a 
sutta. Several suttas in this compilation itself are also 
identical or nearly so. As a rule, I have not made the 
obvious identifications, because I scarcely thought this the 
right place nor could I spare the time and space required. 


VI. 


Like the preceding volumes, this ends with three Indices: 
1. Index of the proper names; 2. Index of the titles of 
the suttas; 3. Index of the gāthās. I wished to add a 
General Index of the proper names and of the Suttas for 
the whole body. But I gave up this not to delay the 
issue of this volume, and have added only the table of 
the Vaggos-Samyuttas and chapters.

INDICES. 


The following are the numbers and abbreviations referring 
to the several Samyuttas. 


XLV. Mag. — Magga-Samyuttam (I). 
XLVI. Boj. = Bojjhanga-Samyuttam (II). 
XLVIL Sat. = Sati-Samyuttam (III). 
XLVIII. Ind. = Indriya-Samyuttam (IV). 
XLIX. Sam. = Sammappadhāna-Samyuttam (V). 
L. Bal. = Bala-Samyuttam (VI). 
LI. Id. = Iddhipāda-Samyuttam (VII). 
LII. Anu. = Anuruddha-Samyuttam (VIII). 
LIII. Jha. = Jhāna-Samyuttam (IX). 
LIV. Ana. — Ānāpāna-Samyuttam (X). 
LV. Sot. = Sotāpatti-Samyuttam (XI). 
LVI. Sac. Sacca-Samyuttam (XII). 
The Arabic, subjoined to these Roman, numbers point 
out the place of the Suttas in their respective Samyuttas 
without regard to the divisions into chapters (vaggos).
